is a member of the IWF Technical Committee provided a unique chance to
ask him to write this useful work. Vorobyev knows the world of weightlift-
ing and the world of weightlifting knows Vorobyev. What he has to say

his field.

<!-- p010 -->

The Author

Arkady Nikitich Vorobyev was born to a peasant family in the village

’

of Mordobo, Tambovsk region, Russia.

His childhood was spent in the small town of Tetusha on the banks of
the Volga, 100 km from Kazan. The beautiful natural conditions of unbounded
forests, fields, the broad and full Volga were the natural spring for the
future strength of the athlete. From the very early age of five to seven he

learned to ice skate, ski and swim.

And when a little older, like all children loved to play football
and gymnastics from dawn to dusk. But these did not have a decisive in-
fluence on his youthful growth and development. In Russia in former times
the peasants amused themselves by lifting a weight in the form of a one-
pound bag. And which child does not want to be stronger than his rivals?

Obtaining a pair of weights, the future champion invited some of
his rivals to a competition: who could lift the weights the greatest number

of times. Arkady soon outrivalled even older children.

But all these games soon came to an end. War began. Studying and
helping his elders was essential. Then began war service. This strong and
healthy boy was taken into the navy, serving in the marines of the Black
Sea fleet. He participated in the liberation of Bessarabia, Romania and
Bulgaria. After the war, again because of his good health, he began work

as a diver.

The sailors soon became very keen on weightlifting. At first they
had an improvised bar - an axle froma waggon, which the sailors used to
while away their spare time from diving. Under the guidance of his first
trainer A.D. Voloshin, Vorobyev took his first steps to the weightlifting

rostrum, in Odessa, in 1946.

Vorobyev was distinguished by his love of work, fanaticism and


<!-- p011 -->

diligence. Ignoring difficult service conditions, he was able to achieve
much in a short time. In the first year of training he secured the title
of absolute champion of the Black Sea fleet, then moved into the ranks of
the strongest men in the USSR.

4

In 1950 he became Soviet champion, which title he retained over the

course of ten years.

Also in 1950 took place his debut at the world championships in
Paris. Then this completely unknown young man showed his "bad character”.
He in no way wished to cede first place to the acknowledged master - Stan
Stancezyk. They both had the same three-lift total of 420 kg, everything
being decided on bodyweight, Stanczyk being lighter.

Vorobyev’s first world title was in 1953 in Stockholm, Then followed
victories in 1954, '55, °57 and '58, and in the XVI and XVII Olympiads in

Melbourne and Rome.

It is very interesting that Vorobyev was not only a sportsman,
although sport evidently played a major part in his life. After finishing
secondary school he entered medical institute, which he successfully grad-
uated from in 1857. Despite the ever increasing training load and depart~
ures to participate in competitions he decided ta continue working on re-
search with the title "The change of activity of the cardio-vascular and

respiratory systems under the influence of the lifting of weights”.

Work was carried out on athletes of high class and was of great
practical value. So, for example, on the basis of many self-experiments
Vorobyev carried out investigations into the mechanism of loss of con-
sciousness during the pression of limit weights, and gave practical advice

for the avoidance of this undesirable phenomenon.

After completing his candidate's dissertation, Vorobyev headed, as
trainer, the soviet national team. Under his leadership the team won in
two Olympics in Tokyo and Mexico, and four times in world and European

championships.


<!-- p012 -->

Vorobyev, working in the capacity of senior national trainer and a
member of the department of weightlifting at the Institute of Physical
Culture, continued to conduct research, connected with the general exper-
tence of the preparation of leading athletes and conducting experiments

if

in the search for new ways of achieving higher sporting results.

This work resulted in the doctoral dissertation, "Medico-biological
bases of weightlifting", which he successfully completed in 1970, and after

a further two years he was awarded the title of Professor.

Vorobyev carries out much general work; he is an active member of
the technical committee of IWF as well as a member of the national Feder-

ation and a member of the Academic Council.


<!-- p013 -->

INTRODUCTION

The value of physical education and sport in contemporary society

The modern technico~scientific revolution, the introduction of new
techniques, complex machinery and automated production, bring about the

conditions for a sharp fall of physical effort in many spheres of human

activity.

There is a decrease in the volume and change in the character of the
physical activity of the majority of people in work and leisure. In the
present epoch the problem of "muscle starvation", the problem of the com-
pensation for the growth of hypokinesis, seen in an unfavourable influence
on physical and mental health, is especially sharp. Together with this,
technical progress, urbanisation and the speeding up of the rythm of life
create a new demand for the increase of the physical preparation of the
incividual, and of his state of mind. In the present-day productive activ-
ity of man a high development of the ability to exchange large amounts of
information of unextended and detailed attention, of a fast reaction, of

precise co-ordination of movement, of psychological stability is required.

Connected to all this is the immeasurable growth of the value of the

participation in physical culture and sport.

Physical culture and sport are social phenomena, conditioned by the

historical development of society, by the socio-economic conditions of life.

Physital culture and sport can be seen as needs of society, of
different groups and of individuals. Participation in physical culture and
sport beneficially strengthens health, increases work-ability, lengthens
creative life. Participating in sport, a person gains satisfaction, "muscle
joy", a better and brighter temperament and optimism in life. Physical
exercise permits the development of a beautiful build, carriage and grace.

Thus a person develops harmoniously, combining physical and spiritual qual-

ities.


<!-- p014 -->

|

Moreover, sport acts as a form of relationship between people ex-
perienced in training and competition. Participation in sport underlines
in a humanistic direction the completeness of the individual. Therefore
today sport plays an important role in the establishment of contacts, good

mutual understanding and friendship between sportsmen, peoples and countries.

Physical culture and sport have been given a high development. They
have received great state importance as 4 major means of physical develop-
ment, improving health, prolonging life, creative activity and also as a

means of popular education.

The development of mass physical culture, for example in the USSR,
the creation by the state of all the nexessary conditions for the practice
of sport is a major factor accompanying the success of Soviet athletes in
international competitions. Preparations for major international compet-
itions, world championships and Olympic games from time to time acquired
a state character. Significant means of support for these are allowed out
of state budgets, various specialists - physiologists, doctors, teachers,
psychologists, engineers ~ are drawn to work with sportsmen, on a special

system of preparation of sportsmen for important international competitions.

World records and high achievements in sport are seen as national
property indicating the stage of physical and psychological development of
the individual. Records and high sporting achievements are the standards
by which the mass af the people compare themselves. High sporting achieve-
ments testify ta the possibilities of the physical development of the
individual, reveal the physical potential of the organism, demonstrate the

victory of the individual over his own nature.

Characteristics of Weightlifting

. One of the most remarkable types of sport is weightlifting. The
practice of weightlifting, like no other type of sport, develaps the
strength of muscles, increases their mass, separation and forms a beautiful
physique. Muscular strength is one of the leading motive qualities and it

is necessary to a given extent for representatives of all types of sport


<!-- p015 -->

and of all people. Therefore in the training of sportsmen in verious types
of sport the lifting of weights, the development of the strength of muscles
is widely represented. :

‘
/

The development of muscle strength obeys definite rules. Knowledge
of these rules, explained in this book, and their application in training

will permit a faster rise in sporting results.

By exercing with weights a definite development is gained by the
vegetative functions and organs. Various psychological qualities are also

perfected.

Exercises with weights are difficult to perfom. The movements of a
weightlifter in lifting a bar of great weight, related to grade, are very
complex as a result of specific muscular activity, of the complicated cond-
itions of their accomplishment, and also in relation to the limiting cond-

itions of the rules of competition.

To the difficulty of performing the classical lifts when lifting
limit weights should be brought: the necessity to produce maximum effort
at definite moments, the control of the points of leverage, everything be-
ing increased to the "pivotless" moment, when during the lift the centre
of gravity of the body is raised, losing the pivoting position in the

period of the "drop” in the Snatch and Clean and Jerk.

Lifting a bar of limit weight nowadays is only possible with an effic-
ient distribution of effort during the performance of the lift, with a
rational trajectory of the lifting of the bar, all of which demands a well-
workedout programme of movements. The psychological factor - "the factor of
great weight” - plays an essential role. In some cases it acquires a more
decisive significance - a lifter who fears a large weight in a contest will
not produce the necessary maximum muscular effort. Also decisive are the
fear of opponents, the fear of losing one's reckognised sporting authority
and the fear of injury. Moreover, the performance of lifts can be made
difficult by the conditions in which competitions are conducted and other

causes.


<!-- p016 -->

It is difficult to know the correction to apply in the course of
performing a lift with a limit weight. Therefore the athlete concentr- -’
ates all his attention when lifting weights on the production of maxi>
mum effort in definite phases of the movement. In cannection with all’
this, it is necessary beforehand to have 4 well-thought-out programme

of the coming action with a list of some possible variations.

The activity of weightlifting aids the formation of certain morph-
ological and functional features of the organism, characterised by the
significant sizes of the weight-height indices. Every weight category
corresponds to optimum heights. If the height of an athlete does not
correspond to the established norm for a given category, then it will
be difficult seriously to reckon such 3 sportsman to be successful in

competition.

“ A peculiarity of the formation of polydynamometric indices is
seen in weightlifters: an advantageous growth is gained by the extensors

and flexors of the legs, and the extensors of the trunk and arms.

The motive apparatus of highly-qualified lifters is characterised
by a high lability, by a capacity for vigorous muscular contraction.
The best Soviet athletes show a short reaction time (0.165 - 0.19 se-

conds).

The energy exchange of highly-qualified weightlifters in cond-

ditions close to basal metabolism are 10 ~ 20 % less than standard.

The energy expenditure of lifters in training is relatively low.
Even under a load of 2000 kilogrammes energy expenditure does not exceed

1500 - 1800 kilocalories.

In early morning rest, highly-qualified lifters display moderate
bradycardia (frequency of heart contractions, on average, is in the range
57 + 7 per minute). Before training the heart rate increases over that of

the morning period by an average of 33 %.

10


<!-- p017 -->

The arterial pressure of highly-qualified lifters in conditions

, of basal metabolism is within normal limits.
é

The majority of electrocardiographical material on highly-quali-
fied lifters in conditions of basal metabolism, is in the range chara-
cterising healthy but non-sporting persons. A significant increase in

the size of the heart is not observed in weightlifters.

The vital capacity of the lungs of a lifter at rest, the minute

respiration volume and consumption of oxygen is linked to weight cat-
: egory. The higher the weight of the lifter the larger are the parameters,
characterising external respiration. Lifting weights is always done

while holding the breath, which conditions the specificity of organic

: changes.

The development of the necessary motive and sychological quali-
ties of the lifter, the perfection of the organism, occurs more success~
fully, when sporting training is based on the knowledge of the normative
reaction of the organism, of all the stimulants to the system, on the
! knowledge of the features of the recovery of the organism after work-

load.

High sporting results, related to world records in weightlifting,
can only be attained by the rational construction of the training pro-
cess, with the use of modern large physical loading, to which the organism

of the lifter can successfully adapt without any pathological phenomena.

44


<!-- p018 -->

, Chapter 1. CLASSIFICATION AND TERMINOLOGY IN WEIGHTLIFTING

Classification of exercises

wf

For convenience in conducting study-training work a classific-

ation exists of weightlifting exercises according to their character.

It is normal to divide all exercises with the bar into classical

‘ and assistant.

The classical lifts are the ones used in international compet-

ition, the Snatch and the Clean and Jerk.

i Assistant exercises are used in training sessions. They are div-
| ' ided into special exercises with the bar and general development exerc-

ises with and without resistance.

With the aid of the special exercises with the bar are developed

and phases, giving rise to the motive qualities: strength, speed, stamina,

H
| the techniques of movement in the classical lifts, of different parts
i flexibility, dexterity.

i

With the aid of the general-development exercises are also devel-
: oped the motive qualities. Moreover, they can serve as a means of active

rest, when changing training or on rest days.

Assistant exercises are classified as follows:-

Group 1 S8uilding and ordinal exercises for concentration, reflexes,

) quickness.

These exercises are performed in the warming-up or closing stages

of the session,

Group 2 General-development and special exercises specified for warming-

up and the closing section of the session.

13


<!-- p019 -->

Group 3 Exercises for the practising and perfecting of the Snatch,

Clean and Jerk, and the development of motor qualities.

Group 4 Exercises for the development of motor qualities. These can ‘be
further divided into the subgroups:~
(a) Linked to the use of apparatus: bar, dumb-bells, weights, discs and

other objects.

(b) Linked to local influence on distinct muscle groups: arms, legs, back,

abdominals etc.

(c) Depending on the regime of muscular activity: dynamic, isometric,

concessive, isokinetic.
(d) Depending on the development of motor qualities: exercises for speed,

stamina, strength, dexterity, flexibility.

Group 5 Corrective exercises for bearing.
Group 6 Exercises for respiration and relaxation.

Group 7 Exercises for the development of emotional control.
Terminology in Weightlifting

Terminology, i.e. a definite system of terms, understood in weight-
lifting, has great value. It is necessary to use generally accepted and
distinct terms and concepts for the fruitful conduct of study-training

work.
A specially important role is played by terminology in the inter-
course of sportsmen, trainers and judges in international symposia, semi-

nars and competitions. Certain requirements are made of a terminology.

Above all, terms must reflect essence and meaning. Moreover, brevity

has great value.
List of Basic Terms
Classical lifts - lifts which make up the programme of competitions in

14


<!-- p020 -->

:
\
i
i
i
i
i
1

ti i getter

weightlifting (the Snatch and Clean and Jerk).

Biathlon - the classical lifts, performed under complex and definite

rules,

Snatch - classical lift in which the bar is lifted overhead onto straight

arms in one movement.

Clean and Jerk - classical lift in which the bar is lifted to the chest

in one movement and then jerked to arms' length overhead in a second

movement.

Start - initial position for lifting the bar from the platform.

Static start - motionless pose from which the athlete lifts the bar.

Dynamic start - a start with a preparatory movement in order to develop

inertial movement of the body and strength of reaction on the feet.
Pull - lifting of the bar from the platform to the ‘drop’.
Full Extension - maximum effort in the pull above the level of the knees.

Pull with Drop - pull executed without bending the legs after the full

extension.

Single Rythm Lift - lift of bar to the drop with emphasis on effort at

moment of full extension.

Opening out - straightening of the trunk and legs after the effort of

the full extension.

Lift from the Hang ~ lifting the bar, which is held in the hands above
the platform.

45


<!-- p021 -->

Check - stopping of athlete after the drop under the bar.

Recovery - standing of the athlete into upright position with the bar

on the chest or arms’ length overhead after a lift.

Split drop - drop while bending the legs, one forward, one straight back.
Squat drop - drop while bending the legs, with ot placed to either side.
Half-squat - drop while bending the legs anly slightly at the knees.

Grip width - width between the hands on the knurling of the bar.

Pressing grip - width of grip suitable for executing the press.

Snatch grip - width of grip suitable for executing the snatch.

Jerk grip - width of grip suitable for executing the jerk.

Grip - method of gripping the bar in the hands.

Simple grip - grip in which the four fingers are on one side, the thumb

on the other side of the bar.

Hook grip - grip where the thumb is covered by the middle fingers.

Thumbless grip - grip where all fingers and the thumb are on one side of

the bar.
Overhand grip - grip where the palms face backwards.
Underhand grip - where palms face forward.

Opposite grip - where palms face in opposite directions.

a

e

16


<!-- p022 -->

ee ee eT

ne ann

Fixation - holding the bar overhead at arms’ length in conformity to

competition rules.

Pause - sho

rt halting of the movement of the bar.

Distortion - lag of the flexing of one arm during the lifting of the bar.

Twist - turning of trunk and bar to one side during lifting.

Arm pull - too energetic inclusion into work of one or both arms in

performing the snatch or jerk, following a slowing down or a pause.

Knee-touch ~- touching the platform with the knee when lowering the body

under the bar in the split.

Leg thrust - help from the thighs during lifting.

Jerk Off - communication of movement to the bar at the start of the Jerk

from the chest.
Knee-bend - movement of knees forward during the lift.

Basic training weight - weight of the bar with which the athlete normally

trains.

Warmup weight - weight with which athlete limbers up before lifting

weights.

Starting weight - weight with which the athlete begins his first attempt

in a contest.
Light training weight - weight of bar up to 70 % of best result.

Medium training weight - weight in the region of 70 - 80 % of the limit

result.

17


<!-- p023 -->

Sub-maximum training weight - weight in the region of 60 - 90 % of the

limit weight. ‘

Maximum training weight - weight of 90 % of limit result.

Average weight in training - relation of total sum of weight lifted in

training in good style, to the number of lifts of the bar.

Volume of load - total weight lifted in training.

Intensity - condition defined by average weight in training.
Coefficient of intensity - relation of average monthly weight to the

result in the biathlon.

Chapter 2. TECHNIQUE OF THE CLASSICAL LIFTS

General Bases of Technique

The sporting results of a weightlifter depend on physical, tech-

nical, psychological, theoretical and tactical preparation.

Technique is characterised by an aggregate of elements and means

of jifting the bar most advantageous to each athlete.

The basic principle of the technique of the classical lifts is -
the lifting of the greatest weight with the smallest expenditure of
force. In sporting technique are reflected:- physical preparedness, indi-
vidual morphological-anatomical features of the organism, technical pre-
paredness of the athlete, as well as age, stage of study of the type of
sport, technical equipment for registering the parameters of movement in

weightlifting halls, level of knowledge of trainer and sportsman etc.

The classical lifts, leaving aside their special difficulties, are

performed by means of a simple movement of the joints - flexing and ex-

18


<!-- p024 -->

tending. The sequence and combination of movements, from which the lifts
are made, their amplitude, direction and speed, define its technical basis.
The basis of techniques stipulates the dynamic initial positions. The
beginning and end of the lift is characterised by a static position. Dyn-
amic positions are intermediate poses, defined by the ending of the pre-
vious phase and the beginning of the succeeding phase of the movement.

These are the boundary positions.

A representation of the rational kinetic, dynamic and rythmic

structure of the classical lifts is especially important for the correct

understanding and study of technique.

Kinetics is the spatio-temporal movement of the athlete, of his

motor apparatus, of his individual joints and of the bar.

The mass of the bar and athlete, and the effect of his strength

are seen in the dynamic structure of the lift.

The rythmic structure is characterised by temporal correlations

of the performance of various components of weightlifting -exercises.

It is necessary to develop the concept of the bases of technique.
This is the rational and correct performance of the most important com-
ponents of the lift. These components are the starting position, lift to
full extension, the drop and, in the Clean and Jerk, also the preparatory

dip, and the jerk off from the chest.

Every qualified lifter has his style, his own individual manner of
performing the classical lifts. But all lifters have the bases of tech-
nique in common. This is that, leaving aside individual morphological
and functional potentials of the organism, at the beginning of the move-
ment the bar is raised with maximum speed, then the effort falls, the
speed of the movement begins to slow down, but in the full extension the
bar is again accelerated. The drop under the bar is carried out by all

lifters with maximum speed. But, despite the common unity of the fund-

19


<!-- p025 -->

amentals of technique for all lifters, they all have an individual capac-
ity for performing this or that component part. For example, ina enntest
not all.athletes develop quickly the great speed of movement of the bar
upwards at the start. This can best be done by the lifter who has elie
tively long limbs and a short body. At the start, their ‘pelvis is signif-
icantly higher than that of a lifter with relatively shorter limbs anda
long body.

Before the beginning of the full extension, in some lifters the
speed of the movement of the bar slows down quite a considerable amount,

while in others such a sharp fall in speed is not noticeable.

For some lifters the effectiveness of completing the whole of the

exercise depends un the vigour of the full extension.

For others it is not essential to perform the full extension with
such a high peak of effort, for here the speed of movement of the bar at

the. beginning of the full extension is quite significant.

The technique of performing the classical lifts does not remain
without change, it evolves and is perfected. On the basis of investig-
ations carried out in our weightlifting department of the Institute of
Physical Culture it is now already possible to speak of a rational, ef-

ficient technique as a standard.

Depending on the makeup of his anatomico-physiological and psycho-
logical features the lifter adopts this or that start, type of grip,
width of grip, style of movement - split or squat - and other technical

elements.

Thus, the advantageous disposition of the levers in the kinetic
chain in different perious of movement is reflected for every lifter in
a rational technique and the optimum application of muscular effort dur-

ing the lifting of the bar.

20


<!-- p026 -->

i
i
{
}

Knowledge of rational technique eases learning and enables an in-

crease of standards.

The Snatch ®

The two hands snatch is performed in one uninterrupted movement.

The bar is lifted from the platform to straight extended arms without a

pause.

The snatch is the fastest movement. Without a preliminary start

and fixation period a single lift can be completed in less than 3 - 4.5 °

seconds.

The snatch is one of the most complicated weightlifting movements.
Lifting significant weight requires of the lifter not only great strength
of the extensor muscles of the legs and trunk, but also precise coordin-
ation of movement, good reflexes, mobility and felxibility in the hip
and shoulder joints, and courage. For ease of study the snatch is divided
into its motor parts (Diag. 1). The snatch is made up of three successive-
ly performed parts:- the start, the lift to full extension, the drop under

the bar and recovery.

The snatch is subdivided into smaller divisions:- seven phases and
fourteen elements. There are two styles of performing the snatch, the
split and the squat. Lifters adopt this or that style depending on their

individual constituent features.

Starting position

There are two types of start distinguishable in the snatch. These
are the dynamic start and the start without preparatory movement - called

by some trainers the static start.

In the static start the lifter up to the tearing off of the bar
from the platform virtually does not change the position of his body and

<!-- p028 -->

its various levers.

This start is adopted by lifters extremely rarely. More widespread
is the dynamic start, which has certain variations. The majority of lift-
ers adopt an initial starting position - the preparatory start. Here the
; angles of the knee and ankle joints are acute. Before lifting the bar from
the platform the hips are raised, increasing the angles of the knee and
) ankle joints. The position which is taken up in the preparatory action at

the moment of tearing the bar off the platform is called the ‘dynamic

start’.

Many prominent lifters, such as T. Kano, J. Davies, A. Kurinov and

Y. Miyake and others, in the starting position before lifting the weight

employed an up-and-down movement of the hips. Rocking forward, to place

the shoulders into the line of the bar, lifting the hips upwards and then
backwards and downwards, and again lifting the hips, bringing the should-
ers forwards while tearing off the bar, is used nowadays by many lifters.

’ But such a start requires good skill, precision and feeling for rythm.

Start at a walk

In the period of the blossoming growth of weightlifting in Egypt
} from the 1930's to 1950's, Egyptian lifters mostly used this start.

The lifter, approaching the bar, set the feet quickly and with the

arms at the sides, placed them on the bar. At the same time, the flexing

of the legs and back, with the hands on the bar, begins the movement. It

demands great training and skill. Today this start is made use of by very

1
|
| F is not necessary to be a specialist in order to know that such a start
| few sportsmen.

\ So, approaching the bar, the athlete takes up the starting position;
setting the feet equidistant from the centre of the bar at the width of
the hips or a little narrower. The feet are positioned parallel or with

the toes turning out. The toes must be in the line of the bar. The major-

23


<!-- p029 -->

Diag. 2. Comparative position of the lifter with the bar

in relation to the position of the feet at the start

Diag. 3. Methods of grip

24


<!-- p030 -->

i
i
|
|

ity of athletes stand with the toes turned to the sides. This creates a

better position for turning the thighs. Morevoer, in lifting the weight,

the bar will be closer to the trunk. Having taken up the starting pas~
ition with the legs, the lifter bends, flexing the legs at the knees and
ankles and placing the hands on the bar. Positioning the feet at hip
width is preferable for the reason that it allows a greater possibility
of utilising the muscular strength of the legs and the height to which
the bar is lifted, at full extension, other things being equal, is great-
er than with a wider foot position. Moreover, as is shown in diagram 2

(a,b) with a wide foot position is seen a division of the strength into

vertical and horizontal components.
Grip

There are three methods of gripping the bar. Simple grip - four

fingers on one side, the thumb on the other side of the bar (Diag. 3a).

The most reliable grip is the hook type. Here the thumb is gripped
from above by the middle fingers (Diag. 3b).

Often, when the press existed, one could see lifters employing the

thumbless grip, with all five fingers on the same side of the bar (Diag.

3c).

In the press the simple grip is often employed, but in the fact
lifts the hook grip is used. The simple grip enables the bar to be pos-
itioned quite close to the wrist, and to be held easily on the chest.

The thumbless grip is advantageous when it is required to have the bar

as close as possible to the wrists. Here the angle between hand and fore-
arm is the smallest. But with the thumbless grip it is hard to perform
the clean with large weights, for it is very difficult to retain the
weight in the hands, and often in this event the bar is torn from the
hands or moved further into the fingers, so negating the advantages of
this grip. Keeping the fingers closed in this grip is hard, which means

it is not strong enough.

25


<!-- p031 -->

1O-i2em

Diag. 5. Height to which the bar is lifted in relation to the
width of the grip

26


<!-- p032 -->

There are three widths of grip, narrow, medium and wide.

Grip width in the snatch has great significance. To establish the
width of grip, it is necessary to bow forward at the waist, while stretch-
ing the arms horizontally at the shoulders. For a narrow grip the angle
between the upper arm and forearm forms an acute angle. For a medium

grip - a right angle, and for a wide grip - anabtuse angle (Diag. 4).

Nowadays a narrow grip is used extremely rarely, because it has

essential shortcomings.

Above all, the height attained when lifting the bar with a narrow
grip is lower than with a wider grip. (Diag. 5a,b). Disadvantageous con-~
ditions are created for the powerful group of extensor muscles of the
legs and back. Very heavy work is transferred to the arms, which, natur-
ally, are not as strong as the extensors of the legs and back. Therefore
a narrow grip is not adopted by lifters. If the best snatches, including
world records, are analysed, it will be seen that the lifters use a wide
grip. But a wide grip also has vital shortcomings. Utilisation of the
arms here is least advantageous. It is difficult to*retain the bar in

the hands during the tearing of the bar off the platform. and on straight

arms overhead.

The angle of the ankle joints at the start must not be excessive,
or in lifting the bar the centre of gravity is moved forwards, and the
lifter is forced to move early onto the toes, which makes it difficult to
hold onto the weight. In the strongest lifters the angle of the ankle
joint at the start is 509- 60°. The angle at the knee joints of different

athletes varies between the wide limits of 45°- go°,

The angle at the hip joints is sharp at the start. The shoulders
are positioned above the bar or a little forward of it (Diag. 6, 4}.
Lift to full extension

Before lifting the bar the hips are raised and the ankle joints

27


<!-- p034 -->

straightened a little. The angle of the knees stays at about 90° or a
little more (Diag. 6, 2). But the angle of the hip joints is reduced
and remains more acute. Therefore the spine takes up a more horizontal

position, almost parallel to the platform (Diag. 6 pts 4, 5).

By the effort of the extensor muscles of the legs the bar is lift-
ed to the level of the knees, which are moved back somewhat. So the ex-
tension of the legs is outstripping the extension of the trunk. The fur-
ther extension of the legs is almost ended. The lifter places his knees
under the bar by means of the transfer of the general centre of gravity
forward onto the toes (Diag. 6 pt 6). The placing of the knees under the
bar is a vital element of the technique of lifting weights. Thus the bar
is found to be closer to the lifter. At the level of the knees or a
little higher, the majority of athletes begin the "full extension’. At

this moment are developed highly advantageous conditions for the effect-

ive work of the massive extensor muscles of the hip joints.

The muscles of the arms play an active part in the full extension
in the majority of athletes. The head at the moment of full extension is
leant back. In the full extension there is a coordinated working of the
massive groups of muscles - the extensors of the trunk, together with
the final extension of the legs, and with the active contraction of the
muscles of the shoulder girdle and arms, by which the bar is given a

sizeable accelaration.

The weight is lifted to the upper part of the thighs with the lift-
er on both feet. The muscles of both legs work to the same extent. In
the final phase of the full extension, the lifter moves onto his toes,
preparatory to dropping under the bar. The bar at this moment is at the

level of the groin or higher (Diag. 6 pt 8).

Split-style snatch

The first movement onto the toes is carried out when the bar is

about the level of the tops of the thighs. It is essential to carry out

29


<!-- p035 -->

7 AREER RRL

OE

Diag. 7. Lifting the bar in the Snatch (drop and recovery)
in the Split style ag


<!-- p036 -->

|

the lift of the bar to the level of the groin with the balance pivoted

on both feet. But, beginning at this level in the split style, the lift-
er moves his centre of gravity onto that leg, which is to be moved for-
ward half a step. But the other leg must travel significantly further,
about two to three times further. This, naturally, requires a longer
time. The shorter is the unsupported phase, the greater is the possibi-
lity of utilising the strength of the legs and back. Therefore, the lift-
er eta bediie his balance on one leg, while the other leg is being moved
backwards, by not more than one-third or half-way. In this phase the bar
still continues to move upwards, by the action of the muscles of the

arms and shoulder girdle (Diag. 7 pt 1, 2).

When the bar reaches the level of the ribcage, the lifter throws
forward the other leg, but by no more than half or one step. Both feet,
thrown forward and back, hit the platform at almost the same time. The
leg which is thrown forward lands on the platform a little sooner. The
drop under the bar can be fulfilled only if the weight is given a suf-
ficient acceleration. For some time the bar moves under its own momentum.
The lifter uses this time to move under the bar. For some time the
lifter is without support (Diag. 7 pt 2). The shorter is this unsupported
phase, the better, the greater is the weight that can be lifted.

The fundamental task in the drop is to place the hips and shoulder
joints under the weight. The difficulty is located in the fact that this
has necessarily to be done very quickly. The bar at this time is located
in front of the athlete. Delay in placing the hips and shoulders under
the bar always erfids as an unsuccessful attempt at lifting the weight,

which turns out to be forward.

During the movement under the bar, it is not possible to maintain
any significant advance upwards. To accelerate the movement under the bar,
the lifter must make use of the weight as a pivot, levering the trunk
downwards onto the forward leg. The extensors of the arms perform active
work at this moment. A combination of the work of the arms with an active

concessive movement downwards is very advantageous for the lifter. But

34


<!-- p037 -->

nostra ht Mines

the majority of lifters do not utilise the strength of the arms in
moving under the bar. They perform the drop without levering the arms’
on the bar. This is disadvantageous, for above all there is not sufé
ficient, control on the bar. In this event, lifters often find them-
selves either too far in front of the bar, which then falls backward,
or the other way about, the trunk is not moved sufficiently forward,

so that the weight and centre of gravity fall forward. It is impossible
to hold the ber on straight arms overhead. During the drop, the work of
the arms helps to control the lifter’s movement under the bar, and its

own movement upwards (Diag. 7 pt 3, 4).

The majority of athletes move the legs forward and backward in
paraliel lines, ie, they set thé feet strictly in the lines from the
original positions. But a more advantageous position of the feet is
made with them turned towards the centre line, toes turned inwards and
heels outwards. The front leg is placed first on the heels and then on
the whole foot. The back leg is pivoted on the toes, the knee slightly
bent, but not touching the platform, Some lifters do not bend the back
leg, keeping it virtually straight, but this is not preferable. The
centre of gravity must fall on the front leg. Then the lifter will
not have a knee touch with the back leg. If the centre of gravity is
positioned on both legs, then it is highly likely that a lifter has a

knee-touch.

Recovery from the split is achieved by extending the forward leg
(Diag. 7 pt 5, 6 }, and then the other, back, leg. But it often happens
that when the bar is held on straight arms overhead, the centre of gra-
vity is found to be forward. Then, only an energetic recovery forward
by means of a rapid straightening of the back leg, permits the fixing
of the weight. This movement sometimes allows a successful completion

of the lift.

Having recovered from the split and replaced his feet ona

straight line, the lifter awaits the command to lower the bar to the

platform (Diag. 7 pt 8).

32


<!-- p038 -->

ee

~ . a o oer et ie Se ey ee re a,

Diag. 8. Height to which the bar is lifted in the drop in
the Squat and Split styles


<!-- p039 -->

Which is the better leg to throw forward, which back?

In the learning of technique by new lifters it should be remember
ed that the forward leg travels the shorter distance. Therefore, it is
an advantage if the balance is maintained longest on the strongest leg.
Here, in the split, all the weight is located on the front leg. This
means it is more advantageous to throw forward the stronger leg, and

back the weaker.

Squat Snatch

The lift in this style is performed by placing the feet to the
side. And so the movement under the bar can be made with a lower pos-

ition than in the other, split, style.

This style can be used above all by lifters having sufficient
flexibility in the shoulders, ankles, knees and hips. A necessity is
flexibility in the spine, especially the small of the back. Without
sufficient flexibility and mobility in these joints it is impossible to

employ this style.

The advantage of this style is that balance can be maintained
significantly longer on both feet, but the weight need be lifted to a
lower height, so that the movement under the bar can be faster and
deeper. Diagram 8 illustrates the comparitive heights of the bar in the

low position of the split and squat styles.

But it is difficult, in performing the squat snatch, to maintain

balance in the fore-and-aft plane.

The starting position before lifting the bar is the same as de-
scribed above for the split style. Lifting the bar to the full extens-
ion is also the same. The differences are located in the drop under the

bar.

34


<!-- p040 -->

yb

Diag. 9. Lifting the bar in the Snatch (drop and recovery) in
the Squat style


<!-- p041 -->

After the full extension, when the bar is at the level of the
lifter's waist, he begins the drop. It is remembered that in the split
style the lifter moves the centre of gravity onto one leg when the ‘bar
gained the approximate level of the groin. But in this style he lifts
the weight maintaining balance on both feet, to the level of the waist,

when the lifting of the bar is practically completed (Diag. 9 pt 1).

The drop under the bar is performed by taking the thighs and
trunk forward (Diag. 9 pt 2, 3). At the same time the thighs are turned
out and the feet placed to the sides. The distance between the feet
depends on the mobility of the lower limbs. The usual position is
shoulder width, but in the event of poor flexibility, a little wider.
Here it must be remembered that the placing of the feet must be done
with the utmost -speed. While moving the shoulder Joints forward, it is
very important that no horizontal movement be transmitted to the bar,
for it would then be difficult to hold a@ large weight. The correct pos-
ition in the squat is: the small of the back arched, feet and thighs
turned to the sides, lower part of the stomach touching the thighs,
arms upright, shoulder blades locked. The bar is positioned behind the
head. The head is inclined slightly forward, the chin can be lowered

or almost touching the chest (Diag. 9 pt 4),

Having performed the drop, the lifter does not remain in the squat.

But utilising the spring of the legs, must at once rise, maintaining his
balance. Recovery with a weight from the squat demands of the lifter
great delicacy and coordination of the whole muscular group which is
involved in raising the lifter. Where there is little coordination, the
centre of gravity is moved forwards or backwards. To maintain the point
of balance under the weight, a movement of the lifter forwards or back-
wards is needed. If forward, the lifter is able to move a few steps,
depending on the size of the platform, but the backward movement of the

lifter is highly restricted.

An attempt to lift the weight, where the centre of gravity is be-

hind the athlete will almost always be unsuccessful. If the lifter has

<!-- p043 -->

weak legs, and he cannot rise from the squat strictly upwards, in order
to lighten the recovery, he can move the centre of gravity forward, and

then recover easier. But here the lifter will make some forward steps.

Completion is the same as in the split style (Diag. 9 pt 7, 8).
If the positioning of the feet is not too wide, the lifter can leave
them in their previous position. But if the feet were placed widely, the

weaker leg is usually brought closer.

The snatch is a fast lift. Especially fast and vigorous is the
performance of the full extension and squat. Here the work of the muscl-

es can be termed ‘explosive’.

The Clean and Jerk

The two hands clean and jerk is the final lift. The highest
weights are lifted in the jerk. Success in a competition sometimes de-

pends on the effectiveness of the performance of the clean and jerk.

Motor structure of the Clean and Jerk

The clean and jerk is performed in two movements: the lift to
the chest - the clean, and the jerk of the bar from the chest to arms’
length overhead. Further, the clean and jerk is subdivided into parts,

phases and elements (Dieg. 10).
Starting position for the clean

The feet are placed at hip width parallel or with the toes point-
ing outwards (Diag. 11 pt 1). The effective performance of the clean
depends largely on a correct starting position. The lifter bends and
places his hands on the knurl of the bar. Grip width is not greater than
shoulder width. The width of the grip influences the performance of the
jerk from the chest. In the case of poor mobility in the shoulders a

wide grip is recommended, even wider than shoulder width. But the clean

38


<!-- p044 -->

6E

Diag. 11.

Lifting the bar to the full extension in the Ciean and Jerk ,


<!-- p045 -->

is made more difficult with an increase of the width of the grip. There-
fore it is very important to find the optimum grip width for every 1ift-
er, which can be done only by experience. i

The hook grip is vital. The toes are in line with the bar, the
projection of the bar on the metatarsal joint. The toes and thighs are
turned to the sides. The size of the angle of the ankle joints is about
70-800, of the knees about 90°, and of the hips 55-80°. The shoulders
are positioned above the bar or a little forward. The head is held free-
ly. The chin must not be pressed onto the chest, nor the head thrown
back. Look forward and dawn. The small of the back is arched and strong-

ly locked. The lifter has his balance on both feet.

Some treiners are against the arching and the locking of the back

in the starting position.

But analysis of the starting position shows that the majority of
lifters who have high standards, always have an arched back before the
Clean. An arched back in the clean is advantageous for the following
reasons:

Better conditions are created for the work of the legs, which are
more effectively used in the beginning of the lift. The movement of the
bar from the floor begins closer to the shins. And during the pull and
full extension very advantageous conditions of the lever relations are

maintained.
The Clean

Before tearing the bar off the platform the lifter raises his
hips (Diag. 11 pt 2). The angle in the ankle joints becomes almost a
right angle, while the angle at the knee joints remains at about 90° or
a little more. The angle at the hip joint remains virtually the same.
But the back takes up a more horizontal position, as a result of the
changes of the angles in the ankle and knee joints. If the shoulder

girdle is positioned behind the line of the bar, then at the moment of

40


<!-- p046 -->

lifting off the platform it moves above the bar.

The effort of the muscles, especially the legs, lifts the weight
to the top of the shins and then to the knees (Diag. 11 pt 3, 4, 5). The
massive muscle group of the extensors of the trunk have not yet taken an
effective part in the lifting of the weight. The higher the bar rises,
the more active is the work of this muscle group. But the most advanta-
geous conditions for the massive contraction of the extensors of the hip
joints are created when the bar is raised to the level of the lower
third or middle of the thighs (Diag. 11 pt &, 7). The knees at this mo-
ment are moved under the bar and located ahead of the grip. The arms are
straight in this position. The bar hangs on the arms. The early bending
of the arms before and in the starting phase of the full extension, is
not advantageous, and can be regarded as a crude mistake. With an excess-—
ively early bending of the arms during the full extension, the massive
effort of the muscles of the trunk and legs leads to the straightening
of the arms, which weakens the advance of the bar upwards. More than this,
the work of the arms gives a directional movement to the bar away from
the lifter, for at this moment the shoulders are positioned ahead of the
line of the bar. Therefore the vigour of the full extension disappears
and the bar proves to be forward. To the massive contraction of the ex-
tensors of the trunk is added the increasingly energetic work of the
trapezius muscles. The head leans back. At the same time the lifter
moves onto his toes. The bar now has its maximum speed of movement up-

wards (Diag. 11 pt 8).

Advantageous conditions for the drop under the bar are thus creat-
ed. At this moment the bar has achieved the level of the loins or a little

higher. The shoulders are leant back.

The lifter moves the centre of gravity onto one leg, the one that
must move forward, and for a while remains as a pivot. The other leg is

freed and begins to move back (Diag. 12 pt 1).

The arm muscles now perform actively. Utilising the strength of

The relation between Body proportions and the Technique of the Classical

Lifts

For the definition of the technique of the snatch and clean and
jerk in practice no account is taken of individual features of body
' structure and the level gained of the motor qualities of leading groups
of muscle. Weightlifters, not taking into account their individual
physical development, learn and perfect the technique of the classical
lifts on a general plan. In this connection, a long time is spent in
the mastery of technique. Because of imperfect technique, the process

of the achievement of high mastery is either lengthened, or it is com-

' pletely unachieved.

Our laboratory has carried out an investigation of the technique

of the classical lifts in relation to body proportions (by A.I. Mul'chin).

An anthropomorphical investigation on 395 weightlifters was

, carried out for the definition of the types of body proportions. Com-
parison of the size of the body showed the presence of a great span be-
tween the minimum and the maximum of the important proportions of
weightlifters of all weight classes. It was seen that on the one hand,
within the limits of one weight category could be lifters of different
heights, on the other, weightlifters with the same height could have
different lengths of legs, arms, trunk, shoulder widths and body
weight. This can be seen in the case of Soviet lifters, who were the

strongest in their time (Tables 1, 2).

Table 1

The importance of height for the perspective distribution of
weightlifters and the average values for each weight category are shown

in Table 3.

Analysis of the discovered body proportions, corresponding to
the average value of height, leg length and arm length, showed that
sharp differences were almost not seen between different groups, for
the differences in the values adduced in the investigations are not
crucial. Obviously, weightlifters of all weight categories can have
different types of body structure, ie ectomorph, mesomorph and endo-
morph. (An ectomorph - athlete with long arms and legs and comparat-
ively short wide trunk. Mesomorph - proportionately built athlete who
has harmonious development of major indicators. Endomorph - athlete

with short arms and legs and a long narrow body.)

Table 3

being impossible by absolute size of the basic indicators of this or ,
that athlete, use was made of the method of relative sizes, with the
help of which was constructed a table of classification of weightlifters

into three groups (Table 4).

The type of body proportions of any weightlifter is defined in
the following way. First are measured height, trunk length, leg length,
arm length, shoulder and hip width, and then is used the formula:

G x 100

F = where G is the length of the indicator (ie part of the

of comparing the attained indices with those in Table 4 the type of body

structure of the athiete is established.

Technique of the performance of the pull by lifters of the three basic
types of build

Sporting technique must be seen above all as the rational organ-
isation of the motor structure of the movement for each sportsman. More-

over, if the given system of movement is envisaged only as a rational


<!-- p061 -->

organisation of all components, then it is necessarily understood not
as a standard, but a norm. Normal technique can have some deviations‘
variations in the kinetics of the movement of body linkages. But i
‘standard’ orders lifters to follow exactly all the specifics of a

model.

This or that deviation of the kinetics of the movement of body
linkages from the average, is conditioned by individual differences:
build of the body, level of the development of the leading groups of
muscles, mobility of the joints etc. With this in mind, the rational
techniques of one sportsman might not be found to be sufficiently ef-
fective for another. Evidently, therefore, in the rational technique
must be reflected the interactions of internal and external forces
and considerations of the given performer. Thus the rational and indi-
vidual techniques of sporting exercises are inseparable one from an-

other, and have very close mutual connection.

With normal mobility in the elbow and shoulder joints in the
jerk the angle between the extension of the arm and the bar is 82°

for an ectomorph, 84° for a mesomorph and 86° for an endomorph.

Normal mobility of joints is a characteristic of weightlifters
who can, at the point of fixing the weight above the head, freely hold

the arms perpendicular to the bar.

Lack of mobility is seen when,- during the fixing of the bar, the

angle at the elbow joints is less than 480°.

Excessive mobility at the elbow and shoulder joints is seen when

in fixing the bar the angle at the elbows is greater than 180°.

With insufficient mobility of the joints the angle between the
arms and the bar must be between 86° and 90° for weightlifters of all

body types.

56


<!-- p062 -->

|
|
|
|
|

In the snatch the following range is recommended for the angle

between the extended axis of the arm and the bar (Table 5).

Table 5
Body type : Size of Angle
Up to Middle Up to Midheavy Above Midheavy
Ectomorph 58 - 59 56 - 58 55 - 56
Mesomorph 59 - 60.5 : 59 - 59.5 56 .- 58
Endomorph 60.5 - 62.0 59.5 - 61 58 - 60

Positioning of the Feet

The feet-at the start for the snatch and clean and jerk are
placed at the width of the hips, with the natural turn of the toes. The
relative position of the bar for the clean is above the feet, so that
its projection is 2-3 cms behind the metatarsophalangeal joints for
lifters of ectomorphic type, for mesomorphs 1-2 cms, and endomorphs
0-1 coms. In the snatch, for lifters of ectomorphic type the projection
of the bar at the start is 1-2 cms behind this joint, for mesomorphs
O-1 om. Weightlifters of endomorphic type place the feet such that at
the start the projection of the bar is one to two centimetres ahead of

this joint.
Size of the joint angles at the start

In equilibrium conditions of the system ‘lifter-bar', and the
work of the motor apparatus of the lifter, at the moment of the dynamic
start, the shoulder joints are seen to have a certain arrangement with

relation to the bar. We will examine three cases (Diag. 16).

With the shoulder joints arranged strictly above the bar (Diag.
18 a} the lifting force F of the athlete is in the direction of the
tearing of the bar off the platform. To move the bar to the shins, the
lifter must develop the additional force f.

57


<!-- p063 -->

US Saag

In cases b and c, the lifting force F is composed of forces
F, = Foos and f = Fsin. The compositional force F, in the direction of
the vertical lifting of the bar, and the force f, in case b, moves';the
bar towards the athlete, and in case c from him. In case b the force f
helps the approach of the bar towards the lifter, while in case c the
development of an additional effort for opposing force f is required
of the lifter. Moreover, in case b, the angle of stability comparative-
ly increases towards the forward edge of the point of balance. This
creates for the system a resistance against the disturbing action of

the force of gravity on the lifter-bar system in the forward direction.

On the basis of calculation and experiment it is expedient, in
the static start, to place the shoulder Sointe a little behind the vert-
ical so the prdlonged axis of the arms and the vertical make an angle
of 2° - 4°, At the moment of tearing the bar off the platform, the
shoulder joints must be positioned strictly above the bar, then the
muscular effort F will be fully in the vertical direction, overcoming

the force of gravity of the system and giving it an acceleration.

In the dynamic start for the clean, a lifter of ectomorphic
type has the angles at the knees at 403°, at the hips 70°, those for a
mesomorph being 93° and 67°, and an endomorph 85° and 62°. In the
snatch, a lifter with long limbs and short trunk has a knee angle of
88° at the moment of tearing the bar off the platform, and a hip angle
up to 54°, an athlete with short limbs and long trunk has a knee angle
of up to 57°, and at the hips the angle is 46°. A proportionately built
lifter has knee angles at the dynamic start of up to 79°, and at the
hips of 52°, Thus the largest value of the joint angles in the dynamic
start are seen in a lifter of ectomorphic type, the smallest in an
endomorphic. In the clean, the angle between the thighs and the horiz-
ontal in an ectomorph is 23°, that of the trunk 49°, those of a meso-
morph are 24° and 44°, in an endomorph 16° and 47°, ie in a lifter with
short limbs and long trunk the angle of the thighs is less, of the

trunk greater.

58


<!-- p064 -->

The Lift to the Knees

It is important to note that with different values of the joint
angles in the poses of the dynamic start, the conditions of work of a
given muscle group are not identical. For example, in the dynamic start
for the clean with a 150 kgm weight, the moment of force in the hip
joints for a middle weight lifter of ecto-, mesa-, and endo-morphic
types, are respectively 61.9, 61.2, 60.6 kgms, and in the knee joints
40.7, 11.3, 11-9 kgms, and in the ankle joints, 9.5, 11.3, 12.8 kgms.
An analogous position is seen in the pose taken up by the athlete when
lifting the bar to the level of the knees. In the dynamic start, the
total moment of force of opposition is smallest in the ectomorph at
82.1 kgms, largest in the endomorph at 85.3 kgms, 83.8 kgms in the meso~
morph. The smaller total moment of force allows a lifter with long limbs
to show great pressure on the points of balance, and consequently to
give a greater acceleration to the bar, than a mesomorph or endomorph.

A similar relation exists in the snatch.

Ectomorphic lifters lift the bar to the knee level in the clean
with an average flexing of the hip joints of a7”, knees 49°, a meso-
morph - le and 55°, endomorph - hips 28°, knees 59°, In the snatch,
for a lifter with long limbs and short body the average amplitude of
flexing of the hip joints is 32°, of knees 58°, for a mesomorph 28° and
63°, for an endomorph 26° and 70°. Obviously, an ectomorphic lifter
has the largest amplitude of flexing in the hip joints and smallest in
the knees. For a lifter of endomorphic type the largest amplitude of
flexing is seen in the knee joints and the smallest in the hips. The
range of movement of the parts of the body is such that the thighs and
shins, in lifting the bar to the level of the knees in the snatch and
clean, have the smallest amplitude in lifters of ectomorphic type, and
in endomorphs the largest. The average amplitude of movement of the
trunk is largest in lifters of ectomorphic type, and smallest in lift-

ers of endomorphic type.
Thus the weightlifter of ectomorphic type in this phese of the

59


<!-- p065 -->

pull completes the lifting of the bar with the accent on the muscles
extending the hips, and a lifter of endomorphic type on the muscles ’
straightening the knee joint. A lifter of mesomorphic type makes use

of the muscles straightening the hips and knee joints in equal measure.

The Lift above the Knees

In this phase of lifting the bar the smallest average value of
movement in the hip joint is in lifters of ectomorphic type (in the
clean 40°, in the snatch 16°). A slightly larger amplitude of straight-
ening in the hip joints is seen in lifters of mesomorphic type, in the
clean 12°, in the snatch 27°. The largest movement is in lifters of
endomorphic type - in the clean 20°, in the snatch 32°, In this phase
of lifting the bar, the knee and ankle joints are bent to the smallest
extent in lifters of ectomorphic type, the largest in lifters of endo-
morphic type. If the average value of the flexing of the knee uae
for lifters af ectomorphic type in the clean is 4° and snatch 40° » in
the ankle joints it is 3° and 8°. Then for weightiifters of endomorphic
type the flexing at the knee joints in the clean is 40° » in the snatch
45°, and in the ankles it is 8° in the clean, in the snatch 14°, The
amplitudes of flexing in the knee and ankle joints for lifters of

mesomorphic type have a medium value.

The amplitude of extension of the trunk in this phase is small-

est for a lifter of ectomorphic type, and largest for endomorphs.

After tearing the bar off the platform the shoulder joints move
upwards and forwards, but the bar moves backwards towards the sports—
man. The movement of the shoulder joints forward of the line of the bar
continues to the moment of lifting the bar to the level of the knees.

Thus in preparation for the full extension the shoulder joints will
move in the direction upwards and backwards. The opposite movement of
the bar and the shoulder joints arises out of the necessity of bringing
closer the centre of gravity of the bar and the bady of the lifter, and

the attempt to reduce the moment of oppositional force of gravity in

en


<!-- p066 -->

relation to the ankle, knee and hip joints. However, the size of this

movement differs (Table 6).

In lifters of ectomorphic type, the amplitude of forward move-
ment of the shoulder joints ahead of the line of the bar is less than
in a lifter of endomorphic type: this determines the character of the
movement of the trunk. If for a lifter of ectomorphic type the trunk
at the moment of lifting the bar to the level of the knees has inclined
in the clean by +6°, in the snatch -4°, then for a lifter of endomorphic
type the angle of deviation of the trunk from the horizontal in the

clean is -9°, in the snatch “00°.

From Table 6 it is seen that the largest movement horizontally
towards the bar by the hips occurs in athletes of ectomorphic type.

In lifters of endomorphic type occurs the largest approach of
the bar to the lifter in the first phase of the pull in the clean, at
4.16% of average height (166 cms), in mesomorphs 4.01%, in ectomorphs

3.71%. In the snatch, these values are 4.34%, 4.10%, 3.86% respectively.

61


<!-- p067 -->

If the relative value of the approach of the bar to the lifter de-
creases with a proportionate increase of the weight of the lifter, 7
then the absolute value of this movement is greater for 4 middlewejght
than for a featherweight, and bigger for a heavyweight than for a :

middleweight.

The size of the approach of the bar to the lifter of various
body types, in the first phase of the pull, defines the position of
the projection of the bar onto the foot in the preparatory start. In
an endomorph the bar is projected closer to the front of the feet than
in a meso- or ectomorph. The consequence of this is that the size of
the approach of the bar in the first phase of the pull is greatest for
an endomorph. So, for some lifters (eg.L. Zhabotinsky) the size of the
approach of thé bar in the opening phase of the pull, when setting

records in the snatch, was up to 20 cms.

Thus, ingnoring the detailed vital differences in the ampli-
tude of movement of the bar and links of the body, the centre of grav-
ity of the bar and the centre of gravity of the lifter come closer to-
gether in all lifters, independently of the morphology of the body.
Moreover, maximum closeness always occurs at the moment the lifter

takes up the pose which begins the full extension.

In comparing the conditions of work of leading muscle groups in
the poses taken up by lifters at the beginning of the full extension,
it has been established that the total moment of force is approximate-
ly equal. But the conditions for work of muscles, the extension of this
or that link of the body. is not equal in amount. For a lifter of ecto-
morphic type the moment of resistant force is greatest in the muscles
that. straighten the hip joints, and least in the muscles straightening
the knees, but in an endomorph it is the other way about. A lifter of
mesomorphic type has the load on all major groups of muscles more equal-

ly divided.

On the basis of experiments it is expedient at the start of the

62


<!-- p068 -->

full extension for the shoulder joints to be positioned ahead of the
bar, such that the angle between the extended axis of the arms and

the vertical continued from the bar is no more than 4°-6°.

It can be seen that the height attained by the bar after the
full extension agrees with the equation H = v’__, ie,is directly

2(g-a}
proportional to the square of the speed of take-off of the bar (and

the force of interaction of the lifter with the bar before the drop),
and inversely proportional to the force of gravity of the bar. Then
the main motive task of the lifter is the communication to the bar of

height, by the necessary take-off speed, as the result of the work of
the links of the body of the lifter.

In the agreement with the theorem of kinetic energy of a body

V =, eeeeeet , where F is the active force, S is the path along which
m

F acts, mis the mass of the body, ie. the speed of take-off of the bar
is direetly proportional to the effort and its path, and inversely

proportional to the mass of the body. Obviously, the speed of the bar
for a constant force depends tn the main on the path through which the

force F works.

The size of the path S in which the lifter interacts with the
bar varies. Lifters of ectomorphic type have the path S in the clean
equal to 25.3% of an average height af 166 cms, in a mesomorph 27.1%,
and in an endomorph 29.1%. In the snatch the path S constitutes 32.3%,
34.2%, 36.2% respectively. The path of the interaction with the bar is
largest in endomorphs and smallest in lifters of ectamorphic type.
Therefore the parameters: of a lifter of endomorphic type have some
advantages over lifters of meso-and ectomorphic type. Here it is necess-
ary to note that, if the lifter of endomorphic type wins in the length
of interaction with the bar, then in the bio-mechanical effort of work-
ing the muscles at the moment of tearing the bar off the platform and

in lifting the bar from the level of the knees, they lose out to lifters

63


<!-- p069 -->

of ecto- and mesomorphic type. There is here a compensation:- losing
in one area, they win in another. Lifters of ecto- and mesomorphic ,
type in the pull to the full extension have a possibility of develop-

ing a higher average force F than the endomorph.

Thus the degree of realisation of the motive capabilities in
sporting results, depends not on the build of the lifter's body, but
on the adoption by him of sporting technique. Rational technique,
matching morphological and functional features of the lifter is the

security for high sporting results.

Theoretical and experimental bases of fundamental positions of the

technique of the classical lifts

The motive act is made up of difficult multi-level activity, of
the phasal movement of limbs, static tension, positional and equilibrial

reflexes.

All parts of the nervous system, central and peripheral, take

part in the control of locomotion.

Muscles are made up of contracting fibres, called a motor unit,
stimulated by one spinal motor-neurone ~ the cx -motor neurone. Muscles,
as is well known, are attached to the corresponding bones by tendons.
In these are positioned the Golgi organs. They are receptors - mechan-
ical or proprioreceptors. The Golgi receptors respond to the total
tension of the muscle. At the same time they are the source of inhibit-

ing signals in the opposite direction.

But, in view of their high threshold of excitability, they cannot

serve as the basic positional links. This function is performed by the

muscle shank. In the muscle shank are a polar contracting section and
a central non-stretching part. The polar part is controlled by the
efferent motorneurones, and the central non-extending part has two types

of afferent receptors, whith fulfill the role of source of return signals.

B4


<!-- p070 -->

Annular-spiral endings lead firstly to primary efferent fibres. The
signals of the primary afferents stimulate excitation in the motor
neurones of the muscles via the structure of the spinal cord. The

Golgi receptors exert a breaking influence upon these neurones. Seond-
ary afferents, in consequence of their small diameter, pass the signals
slowly by the poly-synapse link. It is assumed that the Golgi apparatus

serves as a receptor defence against the overload of the system.

In the control of movement both the central and peripheral parts

function as a single structural system.

The receptors of the muscles which are sensitive to changes of
their length, receptors of the tendons which are sensitive to changes
in their tension, and receptors of the joints which are sensitive to
changes in the position of the limbs. and trunk, all transmit excitations
in the contracting muscles, signals about the degree of contraction of
the muscle fibres. In the central nervous system arise impulses which
are transmitted through the efferent path to the muscles, making them

contract.

Nervous regulation of the motive act is provided with three sub-
systems. The first, made up of motor neurones, muscles and proprio-
receptors, controls the contracting function of the muscles. The second
is responsive to the excitation of the proprioreceptors. The third,
consisting of axional colateral motor neurones, is positioned in the
neurones of the Renshaw cells, controls the self-regulation of locom-

otion. .

‘on

The basic role in the circuit of control of locomotion by the
brain is fulfilled by the functional change of the system of inter-
action of the muscles, and not by spontaneous control of the activity

of individual muscles.

The conditions in which the motive act is completed have very

great meaning. Very often they are complicated by the presence of

65


<!-- p071 -->

|
q

limited amplitude of movement and point of balance, of strictly de-:
fined moments for applying maximum strength and of speed of muscle *
contraction etc. Such complex conditions of motive activity are i

characteristic of the sport of weightlifting.

Every exercise with resistances is characterised by biome-
chanical peculiarities and moreover by specific biological influence

on the organism.

In the performance of exercises with significant resistance in
the contracting act in a short time are involved a large number of
functional muscle units. Massive contraction of the muscles stimul-
ates an anabolic effect, ie. a synthetic process in them. The form of
biological adaptation as a consequence of this is hypertrophy of the
skeletal musclulature. This conditions the possibilities of greater
ease and speed of completing the motive act, for only hypertophised

strong muscles are able to oppose significant resistance.
some general features of the kinetic system of the individual
See Se le Kinetic system or the individual

The various number of possible movements, by which nature re-
wards a person, creates well-known difficulties. They consist of the
necessity of the correct choice of the trajectory of movement. More-

over, the correct dynamics of movement have vital significance.

The following are the conditions for movement which assure the
kinetic system: all links of the body, in all joints, move in curves,
muscles influence the bone-levers variably, for the angle of the pull
af the muscle changes, ie. the force lever and moment of force change.
External forces, applied to the body of the athlete, also affect the
skeletal levers. The action of external forces is transmitted from
One part of the body to the other via bones and muscles. Therefore
the action of these forces changes, if the force itself does not change,

but only the lever arm changes, so changing the moment.

66


<!-- p072 -->

Obviously, the excitation of the muscle also varies, as the
afferent impulse also changes constantly, in dependence on the form-
ations of the peripheral system. In their turn, the corresponding
efferent impulses also turn out to be different, and do not answer

the demands of the changing situation.

For thus it is possible to conclude, that in lifting maximum
weights the whole system of movement of the individual is not suited
for the uniform completion of the aims of the motive act, in which are
involved many links of the kinetic chain. This has a direct bearing

on the examined lifts of weightlifters.

The feature of the kinetic system of the individual consists
in the fact that in every joint there is only one position in which
arise advantageous conditions for the revelation of the strength of
distinct groups of muscles. It is important to know these poses, ie
the conditions of the disposition of various links of the kinetic
chain, for their position reflects on the production of the strength

capacity of the lifter.
The understanding of force and movement

Changes of movement of the body come about under the action of

forces.

The following forces can be distinguished: driving, braking,
neutral. Moreover, they can be regarded as internal and external forces.
To external forces are related above all the force of gravity and
certain other forces which can act on the individual. The weight of
the bar will be regarded as an external force. Internal forces are

ones which arise within the lifter.

The movement of the body of the lifter and also of the different
links of his kinetic chain, occur with positive and negative acceler-

ation. In accordance with Newton's second law, the movement of the body

67


<!-- p073 -->

and the action upon it of forces are interconnected. The movement of '

t

the bar depends on the development of effort by the lifter.

1

In accordance with the principle of optimisation, a rational
technique is characterised by a minimum expenditure of energy at de-
fined moments in the lifting of the bar. This is attained thanks to
advantageous and precise coordinated utilisation of the strength of
the muscles, external forces, inertial forces of the body of the lifter

and the bar.

An important role in lifting maximum weights is played by the
dynamic figure, the rational distribution of effort in concrete periods
of time, ie. the ability to lift the largest weight with a given force.
This fact has received attention only in recent years, when it has

been interpreted.

We now come into collision with the incorrect recommendations

by trainers.

For example, ‘begin the start slowly’ or ‘accelerate the move-
ment gradually’. Such recommendations do not agree with the facts

discovered in recent years.

It is well known that a lift performed with different degrees
of muscular effort is characterised by differences of the structure of

the movement.

A thorough analytical investigation of this question, with the
aim of defining the optimum character of the application of forces,
carried out in our department with the use of methods of mathematical
model-building and also many experiments, showed that the most economic
motive cycle, accompanied by the minimum expenditure of power, is
achieved when it is performed with a definite order of maximum effort
followed by reductions of definite size. Thus in performing the clean

and snatch, the first phase must begin with a mighty, early effort

68


<!-- p074 -->

(140 - 160% of the weight being lifted) in the course of 0.2 - 0.25
seconds in the first 2 - 3 cms of its path.

Following this effort, in the course of 0.4-0.5 sec. it must
be reduced right up to the moment of the full extension to the level
of 100% of the lifted weight, and sometimes for a short while a little
lower (0.05-0.1 sec at 70-80% of the weight of the bar).

The reduction of the effort does not occur continuously, but
undulatingly. As is shown by kinetic analysis, this undulation is
explained by definite laws, for the alternating group and individual
levers of the kinetic chain begin to work dynamically when the vertical
speed of the bar (as the result of the dynamic work of the previous

jevers) achieves a maximum or close to it.

The second phase of the pully the full extension, like the first,
must also begin with a massive effort {160-180% of the weight of the
bar) for 0.14-025 seconds in the first half of the path of the full

extensidn.

i Active influence on the bar ends when the angle of the knee
joints is 165-170", and the angle between the trunk and the vertical
is 15°-20°. At this moment the bar achieves its greatest vertical
speed. With continued straightening the effort of the athlete becomes
smaller than the weight of the bar.

Following from this, the second part of the movement of the
lifter in the full extension must be utilised not for the possibility
of greater lifting of the bar upwards, but for the switching over from
a coordinated programme of lifting the bar, to a coordinated programme

of performing the drop.

Thus in performing the pull there is an alternation of two great
quantities of force (at the beginning of the first and second pheses)

with two small quantities applying in the second half of the performance

6S


<!-- p075 -->
