Diag. 20. Biomechanical characteristics of the Jerk


<!-- p076 -->

in the stated phases of the lift (Diag. 19).

Such undulation of effort is determined, above all, by the dyn-
amic power capabilities of the lifter, ie. his ability to communicate
to the bar the maximum acceleration concentrated nominally in the

first part of the stated phases of the movement.

With such a disposition of effort, the first phase of the pull
decides 65-70% of the motor effort, the remainder being achieved by

the full extension.

It is well known-that the realisation of the mastery of a skill
flows in a definite coordinated movement, in the basis of which lies
the principle of reverse connectian. The receiving elements of this
connection are the muscle and tendon receptors, which signal to the
central nervous system about the conditions of the muscles and the
position of the kinetic links in space, and also about the degree of

utilisation of the motor apparatus.,

On the basis of these signals the central nervous system works
out the necessary corrections, which with the help af the programmed

mechanism corrects and makes exact the movement.

Looking from this point of view at the process of performing the
pull, it can be supposed that the mast important correction of the move-
ment is made in the performance of the full extension, the size of the
impulse of which is determined by the central nervous system of the
basis of the analysis of the parameters of the movemant of the bar in
the first phase of the pull. If the height and spesd of the bar (which
are the fundamental parameters) as the result of the performance of
the first phase of the pull seems greater than needed, then the impulse

of correction will be less, and vice versa.

Thus the effort developed by the athlete in the full extension
is inversely related to the effort developed in the first phase of the

71


<!-- p077 -->

pull. From here it follows that the largest percentage of the real-
ization of the static power potential, in the full extension is not , *
the mark of modern technique, but characterises a non-energetic, weak
performance of the first phase of the pull. At the same time an energ-
etic confident beginning of the movement, with the application of

maximum initial effort, requires a smaller size of the full extension,

and characterises a more modern technique.

Analysis of the performance of the jerk shows that the character
of the movement of the athlete must not be seen in isolation from the

movement of the bar located on his chest.

In performing the lift, the lifter exerts influence on the
elastic bar. Creating a definite oscillation set up at its ends by the
weights. The period and frequency of this oscillation depend on the

weight of the bar, and also on the character of the movement of the

lifter.

In working with the weight of the bar, the parameters of its
oscillation do not depend only on the parameters of the movement of
the lifter. In performing the exercise, external forces (oscillating
movement of the bar, its weight, reaction of levers etc) and internal
forces - the work of the muscular system of the lifter - work on the
system ’lifter- bar’. The most important task of the technique of move-
ment, especially in speed-power exercises with accelerative structure,

is the effective utilisation of reactive and external forces. In pract-
ice it has been found that the solution of the problem of the joint
movement of the lifter and bar, that the most efficient rythm of move-
ment of the lifter which permits the best utilisation of external and

reactive forces is such that the frequency of oscillation of the bar

is twice that of the movement of the lifter.

Some of the parameters of the movement of the lifter relative

to the jerked weight of the bar are shown in Table 7.

The special findings of a rational rythm of movement are the
fast and not too deep performance of the dip, with a sharp pause, and
a short duration wait (up to 0.2 sec) at the bottom of the low pos-
ition. This movement brings out intensive oscillation of the discs of
the bar relative to the point of balance. At the pause the muscles are
working in a ballistic regime, which creates favourable conditions for

the work in the dynamic regime which is following.

The jerking of the bar upwards must begin at the same time as
the start of the upward movement of the discs under the action of the
elastic force of the bar (up té 0.2 sec. after the stop in the dip),
and must have the character of an energetic short-duration jerk, made

by the straightening of the legs in the knee and ankle joints.

As shown by experiments, in the given rythm, one third of the
necessary speed of the bar is achieved through the elastic energy of
the bar, and two thirds of the speed is given by the lifter. There is
reason to suppose that the effective work of the elastic force can be

increased to 50%.

Related to this, the basic dynamic power possibility of the
lifter is concentrated in the opening phase of the jerk (in the first
3-6 cms of the path of movement). It is necessary to give particular
attention to a clear-cut performance of the opening part, so that it
decides the success of the performance of the lift. The dynamic power
potential of the lifter is sharply reduced in the second half of the
jerk-off.


<!-- p079 -->

This part of the movement, too, like the performance of the
full extension, must be utilised by the lifter for the change-over
from the coordinated programme of the jerk-off to the coordinated ,pro-
oT

gramme of the split.

Striving to jerk the bar as high as possible and rising for
this onto the toes, must be considered a mistaken recommendation. An
insignificant rise onto the toes, occurring a little earlier than the
full straightening of the legs, enables a more energetic push of the
lifter away from the bar, with the aim of performing the split with

the utmost speed.

In Diagram 20, for the performance of the jerk by V. Kurentsov
with 175 kgms,.is shown (1) acceleration of the bar, (2) change of
angle in the ankle joints, (3) speed, (4) movement of the bar. The
speed and movement of the bar was noted by an accelerometer (scale in

tenths of a second).

As is seen, the duration of the dip is 0.30 sec, the pause in
the dip is 0.15 sec, the jerk-off is 0.15 sec. The jerk-off begins
after the upward mavement of the discs, when a definite upward speed
has already been achieved, and is accompanied by a reduction of the
effort developed by the lifter ie. occurs in the facilitated conditions,
that the static preparatory tension of the muscles in the preparatory

dip enables a fast and energetic performance of the jerk-off.

It has been found that a fast split enables the lifting of
bigger weights.

With the increase of the speed of performance of the drop, the
Tequired speed of the bar is reduced, ie.it is possible for the lifter
to lift the bar with less speed. But this reduction depends on the abil-
ity to perform the drop. In using the squat style, other things being
equal, the required speed of the bar is 15-35% less than when using the

split. Moveover, with the increase of the speed of the performance of

74


<!-- p080 -->

the drop, the advantages of the squat style increase. This is especial-

ly true of the clean and jerk.
In the performance of the snatch the difference in speed is not
vital.

jifter and bar relative to the acceleration of the performance of the

drop by the lifter of average height.

The basic advantage of the squat is that the lifter performs
the drop lower, and consequently takes a longer time. This gives him
the possibility of utilising the time of reverse movement of the bar,
and to perform the drop with a smaller speed of the bar, and consequent~
ly with smaller expenditures of energy. It is not surprising, therefore,

that today all the strongest lifters of the world perform the drop with

this style.

Over years in our laboratory has been studied the technique of
the classical lifts, with the aid of complex methods, which register
the parameters of movement of the lifter during the lifting of the bar,
af the change of angle of the ankle, knee, hip and elbow joints, of the
bio-electrical activity of the muscles of the upper and lower limbs of
the trunk, of the reaction of the feet on the platform, of the system
‘lifter-bar’, the height attained by the bar, and time characteristics

of various kinetic links and of the bar.

141 - electromyogramme of the quadriceps femoris;

12 - solid electromyogramme of calf muscles.

As is seen from the diagram, rational technique is characterised
by the strictly sequential entry into work and exit of distinct groups
of muscles. The trapezius muscles work actively in the beginning, and
then in the closing phase of the performance of the snatch. The arm
biceps (9) begin active work only in the period of the full extension

and drop, compare honiogramme (1).

The leg biceps, as the extensors of the hip joints, (10), begin
activity 0.1 seconds after the tearing of the bar off the platform and

Diag. 22. Biomechanical and myographical parameters of the Clean

73

t “et (Bets


<!-- p085 -->

In Diagram 22 is shown a model of rational technique of the
clean, by a lifter of high champion class and world record holder

(Vv. U.) the weight on the bar is 195 kgms.

In the diagram are shown the following: -
AJ - ehange of angle of ankle joints;
KJ - change of angle of knee joints;
HJ - change of angle of hip joints;
F - changes of reaction on the feet;

V - changes of the speed of the lifting of the bar.

The change of magnitude of the honiogramme of the knee joints
is divided on the basis of the division of the clean into phases.
First phase is the preparatory, beginning, change of angle of KJ at
the moment of tearing the bar off the platform. Second phase - pull
or preparatory start. Beginning of the phase - the moment of the tear-
ing of the bar off the platform to the first maximum straightening of
the knees. Third phase - amortisation - pushing the knees under the
bar, the beginning of the phase - the first maximum straightening of
KJ to the beginning of the second straightening. Fourth phase - the
full extension - the second maximum straightening of the knee joints.
Fifth phase - the pivotless phase. The beginning of the second maximum
bending of the knee joints. Sixth phase - the active interaction of the

lifter with the bar in the drop.

The technique of the clean of the given lifter is characterised
by a clear and strict sequence of the beginning and ending of the work

of defined groups of muscles.

So the trapezius muscles begin to work actively from the middle
of the second phase and continue to work to the end of the third phase,

and then again work actively in the sixth phase.

The arm biceps begin to work only at the end of the third phase

and continue to work all the time to the middle of the sixth phase.

80

|
|
|
i


<!-- p086 -->

The triceps of the arm display activity in the period of the
first and second phases. Probably, the rise of bio-electrical activ-
ity of the triceps can be explained by passive tension as the result

of the lifting of the weight.

The thigh biceps work in the period of the straightening of the
hip joints in the middle and end of the second phase. Then they active-
ly enter work from the fourth phase and continue to work to the end of
the fifth phase. In the period of the active interaction with the bar
in the drop in the middle of the sixth phase they again show fora

short time activity.

‘

The thigh extensors work, in tension, in the first three phases.
But at the end ef the second phase and after the third their activity
falls away. This period is connected to the pushing of the knees under

the bar. Later the extensors work from the end of the fifth phase to
the end of the pull.

The calf musclesbegin working for a short while from the middie
of the first phase, and display activity to the end of the phase. This
can be explained by the movement of the centre of gravity forward clos-
er to the toes. After this they are inactive. If they had continued
to show active contraction, it would have been a mistake - a too-early
movement onto the toes. The calf muscles again begin actively to work
from the middle of the second phase and continue through the third phase
and for half the fourth.

As shown, the bio-electric activity of various muscles has a
rapid-fire character. Short-duration activity hands over to 'quiet'

muscles, the indication of multimuscular coordination.

The dynamogramme shows, in the clean, in the first phase a growth
of effort. At the moment of tearing the bar off the platform the press~
ure on the feet is 250 kgms, which is 128% of the lifted weight. The

first phase of the dynamogramme is ended by the first maximum effort

A4


<!-- p087 -->

i
|

which equals 285 kgms or 146% of the lifted weight. Then the pressure
on the feet falls, and at the moment of the first maximum straighten- .
ing of the knee joints is at the level of 160 kgms, and at the end of
the second phase is 130 kgms or 66.6% of the lifted weight. The third °
phase of the dynamogramme is characterised by a sharp growth of press~
ure on the feet. At the end of this phase the force is at the level of
290 kgms or 148.7% of the lifted weight. The fourth phase of the dyna-
mogramme is characterised by a sharp fall of effort and at its end the
force does not exceed the weight of the lifter. The fifth phase is pi-
votiess, the force on the platform falls to zero and at the end of the
phase grows again to the weight of the lifter plus the weight of the
bar. In this phase the lifter is completing the drop. The sixth phase

s that of active pressure on the feet. At the end of this phase the
force on the feet is at the level of 345 kgms (176.9% of the weight of
the bar).

The speed of movement of the bar without pause increases ta the
end of the second phase, attaining 1.4 m/sec (V3 ). Then it drops to
4.2 m/sec (Vy }. From the beginning of the fourth phase it increases at
the beginning of the fifth phase 1.8 m/sec (V, }. After this it falls

to nil (Ve). and then has a reverse value.

Changes of the angle of the knee joints (KJ). At the moment of
tearing the bar off the floor the angles of the knees change little and
are equal to g6°. In the second phase there occurs active tension of
the angle of KJ, its amplitude of extension being 7°, and at the mom-
ent of the first maximum straightening this angie is 157°. In the third
phase the angle is reduced through an amplitude of 49° to a maximum
point of 446°, In the fourth phase the angle of the knees again straight-
ens, through an amplitude af 34°, to a second maximum straightening of
472°, By the end of the fifth phase the angle of the knee joints drops
to 82°, and by the start of the recovery to 56°.

The character of the honiogramme of the ankle joints (AJ) is re-

miniscent of that of the knee joints. At the moment of tearing the bar

82


<!-- p088 -->

off the platform the angle of AJ changes little at a value of 63°. In.
the second phase the angle of AJ grows by 24° and at its maximum has

@ value of 87°. This is the first maximum straightening of AJ, which
coincides with the first maximum straightening of the knee joints. To
the end of the third phase the angle of AJ falls by 20°, the maximum
value of the bending tis 67°. In the fourth phase the angle of the ankle
joints is actively straightened, growing by 38°, to the moment of the
second maximum extension, when it has the value of 405°. Here the max-
imum straightening of the ankle and knee joints does not coincide. In
the fifth phase the angle of the ankle joints decreases by 48°, to a
value of 51°. In the sixth phase, this angle has decreased only slight-

ly and at the moment of recovery is 55°,

At the moment of tearing the bar off the platform the angle of
the hip joints (HJ) changes little, and is 69°, In the second phase
the angle in the hip joints grows by 40° to a level of 109°. At the
end of the third phase the angle of the hip joints has changed by 37°
and is now 146°. In the fourth phase the angle of HJ is increased by
30°, to 176°. This is a maximum. But this does not coincide with the
second maximum straightening of ankle or knee joints. In the fifth and
sixth phases the angle of the hip joints decreases, at the end of the
fifth by 66°, reaching a level of 410°, and at the end of the sixth
it is at the level of 60°.

The Jerk

For the ease of analysis of the technique of the jerk, use has
been made of the change of angle of the kneejoints to divide its course
into phases. First phase ~ preparatory. The lifter, slightly bending
his legs at knees and ankles and quickly straightening them, vibrates
the bar, giving it an oscillating movement. Second phase - preparatory
dip. The knees are bent to an angle of 420°. Third phese - jerk off.
The angle of the knee joints is straightened to 482°, Fourth phase -
the split. The angle of the knee joint in the front leg bends to 148°,
then after placing the feet on the platform, the angle in the knee

joint increases a little and then decreases to 120°, Fifth phase -
recovery from the split. The angle in the knee joint in the front leg
straightens to 432°, After drawing up the back leg the angle in the

The angles in the ankle and hip joints during the split change
similarly to the knee joints.

As seen in Olagram 23, the work of the muscle in the jerk dis-
plays a clear sequence of beginning and ending. The trapezius muscles
in the first phase are quiet. In phase two they begin to work after
0.1 seconds, and continue to work almost to the end of the third
phase. After a-short quiet period they begin to work again from the
middle of the fourth phase and work to the end of the lift. The arm
biceps begin working from the end of the second phase and display
activity to the end of the third, and the beginning of the fourth
phase. In the fifth phase in the period of the straightening of the
arms, they again display activity. The arm triceps show activity only
from the end of the first to the end of the second phase. The leg
biceps of the front leg show activity from the middle to the end of
the fifth phase. The thigh extensors in sharp outbursts, work in all
phases. The calf muscles show activity from the end of the second
phase almost to the end of the third phase. At the end of the fourth
phase, there is a short burst of activity. They are then active from
the middle of the fifth phase.

In the first phase of the dynamogramme the force grows to
210 kgms (FA), but in the second phase the force reduces to 30 kgms.
From the middle of the phase to the period of the pause of the move-
ment downwards, the force sharply rises, attaining at the end of the
phase 310 kgms (F3). To the middie of the third phase, the force rises
to 370 kgems (Fa). After the middle of the third phase the force sharply
falls to nil. From about the middle of the fourth phase it grows again,
attaining at the end of the phase 270 kgms (F.). Then in the fifth and

85


<!-- p091 -->

sixth phases the force falls, and in the seventh phase reaches the

level of the weight of the bar plus the weight of the lifter. t
Fi
The speed of the movement of the bar changes in the following
manner. In the second phase the speed of the bar is 1.31 m/sec negative.
In the third phase it grows to 1.92 m/sec positive. In the fourth phase
the speed has a negative value, while in the beginning of the fifth
phase it already has a positive value of 0.15 m/sec. It then grows to

0.82 m/sec.

With a poor technique there can occur a quite significant bend-

ing of the hip joints in the second and fourth phases.

The examined complex method is highly informative for the study
of technique. With its aid it is possible to eliminate mistakes in
technique. But for practical purposes this method is very complex. It
is however very helpful to draw the trajectory of the movement of the
bar. In relation to preparation and experience, by the trajectory of
the movement of the bar, trainer and lifter can not only define mist-

akes, but also correct them.
Trajectory of Movement of the Bar

By means of the trajectory of the movement of the bar (traced
out by its end) it is possible to judge quite fully the quality and
efficiency of technique of the performance of the exercise by the lift-
er. Approximately ten to fifteen years ago many trainers and lifters

tried to see that the bar was lifted strictly vertically.

However, through analysis of classical lifts by prominent lift-
ers, world record holders, world and Olympic chanpions, it was discov-
ered that in all of them the trajectory of movement of the bar was not

a straight vertical line but an S-shaped curve.

Further study of the trajectory of the movement of the bar


<!-- p092 -->

showed that such a curve is more rational and expedient for lifting ay
limit weight. The trajectory of movement of the bar depends on the ;
disposition of various links of the kinetic system of the lifter, be-
ginning with the starting position and continuing to the end of the’
performance of the lift. Moreover, in the trajectory are reflected
dynamic parameters arising from the lifter's effort, from his speed of
muscular contraction in various phases of the lift. In their turn, all
dynamic parameters depend on the disposition of various links of the

kinetic chain in different phases of the lift.

Special study in our laboratory by M.S. Khlystov of trajector-
ies showed that there are various curves in which the bar can be lift-
ed,

Analysis of the discovered trajectories showed that lifters of
different qualification lift the bar in different trajectories. Three
basic types of trajectory of the movement of the bar can clearly be
picked out of all the possible variations of the performance of the

snatch and clean (Diagram 24).

The first type is the trajectory where, after the moment of
tearing the bar off the platform, the bar first approaches the lifter
in the first phase of the pull, then intersecting the vertical line
drawn through the starting point of the movement of the bar, maves away
from the lifter upwards, in the second phase of the pull, and again
intersects the vertical line. The ending of this trajectory is a
"hook! of definite width at the moment of the performance by the lift-
er of the drop and the fixation of the bar on straight arms in the

snatch, and in the clean, on the chest.

In Diag. 25 are shown three basic variants of the first type of
trajectory. The basic distinction one from another relates to the dis-
position of the upper part of the trajectory in relation to the vertic-—
al line. This type of trajectory characterises rational technique with

an advantageous disposition of various kinetic links throughout the

88

relating to the first type

whole exercise.

The second type of trajectory is such that the movement of the
bar as in the first type. begins by moving towards the lifter, attain-
ing maximum movement at the moment of pushing the knees under the bar
(first phase of the pull). In the second phase of the pull the bar
approaches the vertical but does not intersect it. The trajectory ends

in a hook of definite width.

In Diag. 26 are shown three basic second-type trajectories. The
basic distinction in this case consists of various sizes of movement of
the trajectory from the vertical in the second phase of the pull, and
also of the disposition of the upper part of the trajectory in relation
to the vertical: The second type of trajectory is never displayed by
rational technique, for in the final stage of the performance of the
lift, the shoulders move backwards too early, especially in variant c,
and so cannot make use of the strongest and most massive groups of
muscles in the necessary manner, which take part in the extension of
the hip joints. It is true that up to the fuli extension the traject-

ory of the bar is rational.

The third type of trajectory is when, at the moment of tearing
the bar off the platform, the bar begins to move away from the lifter
(from the vertical}, then intersects the vertical, approaching the
lifter in the first phase of the pull (Diag. 27}. There further occurs
a repetition of the movement away from the lifter and the curve again
intersects the vertical. Higher, it for the third time cuts the vertic~
al, ending like the other two types in a hook of definite width. This
type of trajectory is characteristic of the situation when in the start-
ing position the bar is placed close to the ankle joints, from which
the beginning of the movement has a direction away from the lifter.
Moreover, the shoulders in the period of the full extension are locat-
ed only slightly ahead of the bar, which does not allow, in the necess-
ary manner, the use of the strength of the muscles which take part in

the extension of the hip joints. The period of their effective work is

trajectory at the beginning of the lift

<!-- p099 -->

short.

a

The position of the bar relative to the ankle joints is re,"

Hf
flected in the form of the curve. In Diag. 28 is shown four basic ’
variants of trajectory, seen in all types, and differing in the way
the lifting of the bar from the platform begins. With the projection
of the bar onto the metatarsal toe joint (Diag. 28 a) the curve
approaches the lifter. In case b the bar is positioned closer to the
ankle. The lifter, as the result of a great inclination of the shins,

must begin to lift the bar in a direction away from himself.

In case d, where the bar touches the shins (irrational pos-
ition) the lifter is in fact forced to roll the bar away from him-
self to the position which creates more favourable conditions for

lifting weights.

In case c the bar is placed far away from the ankles, therefore
the lifter must roll the bar towards himself, which is irrational. Of

all the four variants the most advantageous is case a,

In Diag. 29 is shown four basic variants of the movement of the
bar, distinguished one from another by their form at the moment of the
closest approach of the bar to the lifter in the first phase of the
pull.

The first variant has a characteristic smooth bow-shaped bend

of the trajectory with a following smooth intersection of the vertical.

The second variant is distinguished by a sharply bending tre-
jectory, and the third by an even sharper, forming an appreciable

‘sinking’ curve.

The characteristic feature of the fourth variation is the move-
ment of the bar at this moment in a straight line (for ten cms or more)

and with a following sharp bend of the trajectory. The bending of the

<!-- p102 -->

trajectory depends on the disposition of the shoulders during the
lifting of the bar. The further they are in front, and the closer is:
the bar located to the body during the full extension, the more sign-
ificant is the bend, Moreover, the height of the lifter is important.
The greater the height, other things being equal, a greater horizontal
movement of the trajectory, in the examined phases, is made possible.
In case c, the sharp bend in the trajectory is created by hitting the
bar with the thighs.

In the second: phase of the pull, after the intersection of the
vertical by the trajectory, the following variants of the trajectory
are seen (Diag. 30). In the first case the trajectory intersects the
vertical, and is lifted strictly upwards in a shallow curve, in the
second, in a more rounded bow-shaped line, in the third case the tra-
jectory is shown in case a, where the lifter in this important part of
the bar’s path, maintains effort in a direction strictly upwards. With
this aim, effectively using the muscles extending the hip joints, the
shoulders in this case move strictly upwards for some time. In case b,
the lifter for a short while makes use of these muscles. In case c, the
shoulders are early thrown backwards - the most irrational way of lift-

ing the bar.

In Diag. 31 are represented three variants of the 'hook’, dist-
inguished by their width and disposition of the higher part of the tra-
jJectory. In case a, the upper part closely coincides with the vertical,
in the second and third cases the upper part of the trajectory is loc-
ated to the left or right of the vertical. The most rational position
is in case a, where the horizontal transference of the 'lifter-bar'
system is a minimum, and the basic effort of the lifter is directed
to the lifting of the bar. In cases b and c the position of the should-
ers is respectively forward and back. It is necessary in both cases
to expend extra effort for the maintenance of balance and the complet-
jon of the fixation of the weight. The smaller is the 'hook', the small-
er is the horizontal transfer, the better is the possibility created

for.the successful completion of the exercise.

Diag. 32. Trajectory of the bar in the Jerk


<!-- p105 -->

In Diag. 32 are shown basic parameters of the trajectory of
movement of the bar in the jerk.

’
7]

Tr the diagram, point 'A' is the position of the bar on the
chest before the performance of the lift. 'B’ is the position of the
bar at the lowest point of the dip, 'C' is the highest point of the
trajectory of the bar in the jerk, 'D’ is the point of fixation in
the split position. The broken line is the vertical projection through
the point of the bar in the-position of the chest before the perform-

ance of the lift.

In the diagram under figure 1 is shown an ideal trajectory in
the jerk. The preparatory dip AB is performed with very little for-
ward translocation, which is almost unavoidable, following the bending

of the knees and ankles. The depth of the dip is about 12 cms.

The jerk off, BC, is performed almost vertically. The variation
from the vertical at point 'D’ in the split is not great, giving

evidence of a small horizontal movement of the bar.

Diagrams 2 and 3 attest to a sharper horizontal movement of
the bar, both in the period of the preparatory dip AB, and in the
period of the jerk off BC. The point of fixation 'D' stands far away

from the vertical line.

To hold onto the bar and complete the lift, as is shown in in~
vestigations, is very difficult if the horizontal movement is greater

than 10 centimeters.

Thus rational technique in the jerk is characterised above all
by a maximum closeness of movement in the dip and jerk off to the
vertical line. And also, the speed of the stop, or the braking of the
movement of the 'bar-lifter’ system downwards, and the utilising of
the elastic quality of the bar, which permits a more massive jerk of

the bar upwards.

Diag. 35. Varieties of disposition of the feet in relation to their

<!-- p109 -->

A rational technique of the jerk is also characterised by the

performance of the split with maximum speed.
irregularity of movement of the bar

The coincidental registration of the trajectory of movement of
the two ends of the bar in the performance of the classical lifts (with
90% weights) shows that the left and right sides of the bar during the

snatch and the clean and jerk do not move in the same directions.

Conduct of a comparison between the trajectory of the left and
right sides, showed that they have completely different parameters
(see Diag. 33). Of all possibilities are registered three basic variants
of the movement.of the bar in space in the horizontal plane. Unevenness
in the lifting of the bar is a condition of the assymetrical develop-
ment of the skeletal musculature of the left and right sides of the
body of the lifter, and also the difficulty of diagnosing the mistake
by sight without specialised instrumental study. Therefore the majority
of trainers and lifters do not suspect that in lifting the bar use is
not made in equal amounts of the strength of the muscles of the left
and the right sides of the body. Moreaver, the position of the feet

and of the bar relative to the ankles has great importance.

Carrying out an analysis of the registered initial positions of
the feet in the start before the performance of the classical lifts
for lifters of varying qualifications permitted the picking out of
two basic dispositions of the feet (Diag. 34) - (a) with toes turned
out, (b) with toes placed parallel with one another. In both variants
was seen a distance between the feet of 15-25 cms for narrow placement,

25-35 ems for medium, and 35-45 cms for a wide position.

During the performance of the classical lifts four basic types
of the disposition of the feet on the platform are registered in Die-
gram 35:- (1) the feet, from the initial position, after the perform-

ance of the lift are disposed strictly to the sides, not changing from

104

ee TT

ssuastieentulis


<!-- p110 -->

i
i
i
H

Diag. 36. Dependence of the trajectory of movement of the bar on the

position of the feet at the start and during the performance of the lift

105


<!-- p111 -->

the previous pivotal planes

(2) the feet, after the performance of the exercise, are moved from = /
the initial position forwards; A
(3) after the performance of the lift, the feet are moved from the ,
initial position backwards;

(4) the feet are moved in different directions - the right forward,

left backward, or vice versa.

Such variations of movement of the feet in relation to their
initial starting position, are connected to the necessity of maintain-
ing equilibrium. If the feet are moved forward, it means the centre
of gravity of the 'lifter-bar’ system has moved forward. If back, then
the centre of gravity is situated backwards. The positioning of the
feet forwards-backwards is characteristic of an unevenness of lifting

of the bar.in the horizontal plane.

Simultaneous registration of the movement of the feet on the
platform and of the trajectory of movement of the bar shows that the

former is in an essential way linked to the latter.

In Diag. 368 are shown three variants of trajectory relating to
the character of the starting position and movement of the feet, during
the performance of the snatch and clean. As seen in the diagram, a
close positioning of the bar to the ankles leads to a curving of the
trajectory forwards. On the other hand, a distant positioning of the
bar in relation to the ankles tends toward the movement of the tra-
jectory backwards. However, it must be seen that the starting position
of the bar in relation to the lifter, and also the succeeding movements
of the links of the kinetic system, especially the shoulders, have im-
portant significance. Their excessive leaning forward conditions the
bending of the trajectory in that direction. And, the other way about,
the premature throwing of the shoulders back leads to the movement in

that direction of the trajectory of the bar.

On the basis of the given instances it is possible to make the

4N8


<!-- p112 -->

deduction that various causes influence the parameters of the tra-
jectory of movement of the bar. Knowledge of these causes permits the
assimilation of a more rational technique, or its perfection in con-

nection with the individual features of the lifter.

Mistakes encountered in the performance of the classical lifts and

their elimination
bet eee Se

Mistakes in the performance of the classical lifts are seen
in all lifters, they are inevitable. In some lifters they are formed
as the incorrect learning of technique, and are firmly strengthened in

the process of coaching and systematic training.

The correction of these mistakes and the rebuilding of a firmly

established stereotype movement is a highly difficult task.

In other lifters mistakes spring up periodically, as the re-
sult of the use of large loads and insufficient attention to the per-
fection of the technique of the classical lifts. With the growth of
training experience, the technique of the movement of the weightlifter
is perfected. Qualified lifters often in their training give greatest
attention to the development of strength by assistance movements,
which, evidently, are not performed the same way as the classical lifts.
The strengthening of skill in these assistant movements is carried
over into the performance of the classical lifts. Therefore training
with the aim of developing muscluar strength must periodically altern-

ate with training with technical performance exercises.

Mistakes arise not only from incorrect study and assimilation

of technique, but also from the condition of the organism of the lift-

er.

Often mistakes arise as a result of fatigue or overtraining. It
sometimes happens that in training a lifter correctly makes use of

exercises, and suddenly in one session arises a series of mistakes,

407


<!-- p113 -->

sometimes not even typical of the lifter. It is clear that in this
condition it is useless to work for the correction of mistakes. /
Mistakes can also arise because of the unhealthy condition of some ',

group of muscles, ligaments etc.

In competition and exhibition matches mistakes are often the

result of strongly marked emotional stimulus.

Before setting about the correction of mistakes the lifter must
find the main cause of incorrect lifting. It follows that the correct
ion of the initial basic mistake sometimes influences the correction

of the other, secondary mistakes.
Mistakes in the ‘Snatch

(1) The bar is ee straight arms, but turns out to be for-
ward, completely fixing the bar is impossible.

Cause: Early movement onto toes, incomplete extension of trunk, pre-
mature drop. Also, insufficient movement of hips and trunk forward

under the bar.

Correction: Lifting of the bar to the level of the middle of the

thighs to be carried out on the full soles of the feet. Drop to be
performed only after the full extension of the trunk, transferring

hips and shouldere under the bar.

(2) Moving backwards with the bar.

Cause: Too early straightening of the trunk, lifter tries, with arm

strength, to lift in the closing stage of the exercise, the weight to
the necessary height.

For lifters who use the split style, drawing the bar towards
himself, moving with it backwards, combined with early straightening
of the trunk and the freeing of the back-thrown leg. The full extension
is carried cut with balance on one leg. Because of this the massive-

ness of effort falls.

Correction: To snatch from the hang and standing on a block.

108

1 bebbeinasceblavit adore ei ab bash at eR lf kta iene UO be BAS ag gD ttc Osage hi SOND NEA Sala ASL NALS SAN aa Ne Re Aen) A a nate Ot te ANA USNA  Lsub anailhonteen meee =


<!-- p114 -->

Cause:

(3) The bar is lifted far away from the shins in the opening phase .

of the lift. =
In the starting position the shins are well inclined, the angle

of the ankle joints is very sharp, and the bar is positioned too far

from the lifter.
Position the bar closer to the ankle joints.

(4) Lifter, during the full extension, hits the bar with his thighs.
The movement of the bar proceeds very close to the shins, knees-and
thighs. The lifter sufficiently quickly straightens the legs, but not
fully, and the trunk is still maintained in a leaning position. To-
gether with an energetic straightening of the trunk, the thighs move
forward, and the lifter hits the bar with the thighs.

To correct the mistake it is possible to bend the shins more
at the start, then the bar in the lift will move further from the lift-

er. The full extension is performed higher, with a smaller movement of

the trunk and thighs forward.

(5) The athlete is not successful in moving under the bar.

Cause: Insufficient muscular strength, too great a weight. Bar is

lifted to an inadequate height. No upward advahcing movement of the

bar due to momentum. The lifter lifts the bar high almost to a full
ending of the movement. There 4s no time for the drop. Moreover, perhaps
there is a leg in the drop and it is performed slowly. A feasible

weight must be lifted to an optimum height. The drop must be made

when the bar still has an inertial forward movement. The heavier the

weight the faster must the drop be performed.

(6) A jump off the floor in the drop.
Cause: The lifter in separating the legs lifts his feet high. The time
of the pivotless position is increased.
Correction: It is necessary to maintain the pivot longer, to position

the feet as fast as possible, endeavouring to reduce the time the feet

are off the platform.


<!-- p115 -->

(7) The bar drops behind the head.
Cause: At the start the bar is closely positioned to the ankles. ;
Significant horizontal movement of the bar (made most often by the ./
strength of the arms). The lifter excessively moves the hip and should-
er joints forward.

Correction: At the start position the feet -so that the projection of
the bar is located on the metatarsal-phalangeal joint. The bar must be
lifted from the level of the upper part of the thigh strictly upwards
without any significant movement in the horizontal Plane. In the drop

the shoulders are moved exactly under the bar.

(8) In the split style, the trunk retains a forward inclination,
stomach touching the thigh and the hips held back.

Cause: The lifter in performing the pull, does not fully straighten

the trunk, and not pivoting on the bar, does not carry the hips forward,
Correction: It is necessary to lift the weight to the full strai ghten-
ing of the legs and trunk with the carrying of the hips forward. The
trunk in the drop must be held upright.

(9) Knee-touch in the split,

Cause: The centre of gravity is located on the rear leg.

Correction: It is necessary for the fundamental part of the weight to
be disposed over the forward leg. The back leg is only slightly strain-

ed and mostly maintains the balance of the athlete.

(10) The bar is lifted in a single tempo. The beginning of the lift
of the bar is not active, further part of the lift is completed with-
out a sharp rise of speed. ,
Cause; Lack of knowledge of change of rythm of the lift. The lifter
begins the lift with low intensity, in which event, highly frequently,
the bar moves away from the lifter.

Correction: The lift of the bar to the level of the knees must be
performed with great effort, then occurs a fall in effort, from the

Knees and higher the massive effort has an explosive character.

110


<!-- p116 -->

ET

Cause: To

(11) Press-out of the bar 4n the drop or in the recovery from the

drop.
o little height of the lifting of the weight. Delay in the

drop. Slow drop. Delay in the work of the arms.
Correction: Depends on the cause. It is helpful to perform the snatch
correct.-—

from the hang, on a4 block, the snatch from an upright trunk position

using only the arms and the drop.

’

(12) Movement of the lifter to the side of the forward leg in the

split.

Cuase: The lifter frees early the leg which is moved back, maintain-

ing balance on the other. In lifting the weight the greater part is
taken by the muscles of the side of the body on the forward leg, which

the lifter does not succeed in moving out to the required distance.

Correction: It is necessary to perform the full extension with the

maintenance of balance on both feet.
Mistakes in the Clean

(1) Bar turnsout to be forward.

Some possible causes: (a) Early movement onto toes before the bar
reaches the knees.

(b} projection of the bar is positioned ehead of the metatarsal-phal-
angeal joint, with a great inclination of the shins in the starting
position, therefore in lifting the weight the bar has to round the
knees, ie. the bar turns out to be in front of the lifter.

(c) The lifter does not fully straighten the trunk and legs, does not
pivot against tha bar.

{d} Arms bend early at the elbows.

(e) Elbows are taken backwards early in the full extension.

(f) Lifter does not carry the hips forward.

Correction of mistakes: Position the bar closer to the ankle joints,
the shins at the start must make a shallower inclination, the angle
at the ankles must not be less than 60-70". The lift of the bar is
done with the balance on both feet almost to the closing phase of the

441


<!-- p117 -->

full extension. The full extension is made with a fully straightened
trunk and legs and with a carrying of the hips forward and down, The ,
elbows must move strictly upwards parallel with the trunk almost to

a

the beginning of the drop.

(2) Throwing the bar, and moving with it, backwards in the split.
This mistake arises mainly because the lifter early frees the leg
and moves it backward. The final part of the full extension is carried

out with the balance on one leg and utilising its strength.

Correction: The full extension is to be performed with the balance on

both feet, only later freeing the back-thrown leg.

(3) The bar is lifted far away from the lifter.

Cause and correction as in the snatch.

(4) Movement with the bar to the side.
Cause: Crossing of the feet during their positioning forwards and back-
wards, with the pull performed with balance mostly on one leg.

Correction: Correct positioning of the legs and maintenance of balance.

(5) Weak full extension.

Cause: Insufficientiy strong muscles, participating in the full extens-
ion, too great a weight. Lifter does not place the knees under the bar.
The bar moves far away. No agreement in the work of the muscles extend-
ing the trunk and legs, flexing the arms and trapezius.

Correction: Depends on cause.

(6) The bar is on the chest, but the elbows are back.

Causes: The lifter does not have time to turn forward the elbows, so
that the weight is located forwards. Or the lifter delays the drop and
the bar does not have an upward movement via momentum. The lifter can
also throw the trunk far back, then have to use significant arm strength
to position the bar on the chest, but the elbows remain situated back.
Correction: The bar must be lifted as close as possible in the drop,

de when the bar has a speed of movement upwards.
